ParlaMint-SI_2014-01-31-SDZ6-Redna-21.ana.u152	Thank you for your word. Mr President, Members and Members of Parliament! You have before you the Interlocutory report of the Commission of Inquiry on the determination and assessment of the operation of extremist groups in the Republic of Slovenia. The interim report summarises the work of the Commission of Inquiry, which carried out the first half of a specific parliamentary inquiry within ten months at seven meetings. The first part was aimed at obtaining quality findings and assessments of the actual situation that could be the basis for amending legislation, preventing the operation and control of extremist groups, namely legislation governing the establishment of parties, associations and other voluntary associations, public gatherings and public assemblies, the powers of security and intelligence services or the amendment of other legislation and other decisions in accordance with the constitutional competences of the National Assembly. The relevant parliamentary inquiry was ordered by the National Assembly at the end of January last year and, at the same time, in early February it also established an investigative commission which immediately undertook a large-scale work. The 89-page interim report represents the first phase of the parliamentary investigation, namely the preparatory investigation process and the implementation of the evidence. On the basis of the decision taken to carry out the preparatory investigations and its two additions, we have received documentation which was the basis for the preparation of the interim report as well as the basis for carrying out the evidentiary procedure carried out within the framework of the evidentiary decision adopted and its completion. Within this preparatory investigation phase, we requested and received material from 9 bodies summarizing the state of extremism in the Republic of Slovenia. We also took note of the research task on extremist organisations, their functioning and the regulatory arrangements in 15 countries. In so doing, let me mention that all publicly available materials from the requested authorities have been attached to the interim report. Within the evidence procedure, the Commission of Inquiry listened to statements made by 6 expert witnesses interviewed. The purpose of the hearings was to obtain explanations of the facts, circumstances and assessments of the actual situation of the appearance and functioning of extremist groups in the Republic of Slovenia. Following 6 meetings, the Commission of Inquiry decided to consider the draft interim report at its 5th regular session. In preparation of the draft, Dr. Peter Umek from the Faculty of Security Sciences of the University of Maribor helped us professionally. His contribution to the prepared material of the essential findings is enormous, so I would like to thank him for the work he has done here. In accordance with the second paragraph of Article 23 of the Rules of Procedure on Parliamentary Inquiry, the interim report also sets out a separate opinion from three members which challenged both the eligibility of the parliamentary investigation, the course of the investigation and the conduct of the evidence, in particular the hearing of witnesses, certain expert witnesses, as well as the content of the report or the essential findings. In short, the three members of the Commission of Inquiry disagree with the parliamentary investigation and the work of the Commission or the course of the investigation and therefore disagree with the interim report. Finally, they reinforced their disagreement by voting against the interim report. I will continue to focus on a brief presentation of the main part of the interim report, which will also be the subject of the decision-making session of the National Assembly. The main findings found on pages 41 to 54 present to us in a concentrated way the legal regulation of extremism in Slovenian legislation and the emerging forms of extremism and extremist groups. Within the set of good 10 pages is followed by the definition of extremism, in the last part, from page 48 onward, we find the main findings from the selected material and opinions of the expert witnesses, as well as the answer to the basic question, by order of the parliamentary inquiry, how to prevent and sanction extremist activity in Slovenia with proposals to amend the current legislation. At the beginning of the main findings, we find that the comprehensive problems of extremism in the Republic of Slovenia have been avoided by all the authorities for almost two decades, leaving open the question of the existence of extreme groups in our country and thus allowing for a wide range of action and inviolability of these groups, all on behalf of the right to unification and freedom of speech. Based on the material collected so far and the testimony of expert witnesses, it is possible to establish that all national security authorities and expert witnesses determine the existence of extreme groups in Slovenia, both left and religious. In addition, it should be borne in mind that the current social and economic situation has an important impact on the functioning and activities of extremist groups throughout the European Union, where they are increasingly active. Extremist groups in Slovenia, according to the competent authorities, currently pose a low level of threat to national security. Although the Slovenian legal order also contains provisions that provide comprehensive constitutional protection against discrimination, prejudice, violence and hatred, most of the professional public notes that the legislation in force does not allow for preventive control of action and effective action against the activities of extremist groups that promote the ideology of racism, xenophobia, anti-Semitism, homophobia. International practice also shows that the tools now available to Slovenia are not effective enough. Therefore, in view of the difficulties facing the phenomenon of extremism in Slovenia, an intervention in existing legislation should be considered and, above all, the practice of detection, prevention and effective prosecution or punishment should be applied in this area. In a brief review of the legislation, the Commission of Inquiry is laying down legal arrangements related to the emergence of extremism in six legal acts: the Constitution, the Penal Code, the Law on the Protection of Public Order and Peace, the Law on Society, the Law on Religious Freedom and the Law on Media. When looking for the definition of extremism, the commission of inquiry is first trying to present extremism and extremist movements. In doing so, she found that we do not have a generally established and identifiable definition of extremism in the Republic of Slovenia. The latter means that the competent authorities approach separately, each in its own way to deal with this phenomenon and the prosecution of the crimes committed by individual members of extremist groups or extremist groups. In doing so, it should be noted that, apart from terrorism, the remaining crimes of extremist groups are not at all persecutable within the so-called association. The Investigative Commission considers that everyone, even a single case, is too much and that it is a matter of endangering the safety of the whole society and not just the affected individuals, since the objective of extremist groups is not only individuals but also the social structure as such. This threatens the whole society, and in general, it makes sense for these groups to prevent or immediately sanction their operation. In the case of extremism, it is not the world view, it is the legitimate political beliefs, it is the actions committed against the rule of law and democracy that undermine constitutional and social order. It should be distinguished from radicalism. Extremism is expressed as a relation to accepted values, rules and behaviours. Extremism has several different faces and is also different in intensity. In the interim report we then briefly presented left, right and religious extremism. We then gave special emphasis to the so-called enemy speech. In doing so, the Commission notes that a democratic and tolerant society must allow its members to express and defend, publicly and privately, a variety of views and ideas, even though they seem provocative, stupid or even offensive. In cases where views and ideas affect the rights of individuals, society must restrict and protect the rights of individuals. The latter restrictions and incrimination should also apply to public encouragement to the hatred, violence or discrimination of an individual or a group of persons where there is a clear risk that expressing or defending views will escalate into violence. Finally, the Commission of Inquiry notes that in Slovenia the problem of hate speech is also related to the rhetoric of some political officials, and is spread by the media. The commission of inquiry then offers the definition of extremism. Extremism is an expression that denotes activities that are morally, ideologically or politically contrary to the written laws and unwritten norms of the country; which are extremely impatient with others, reject democracy as a means of managing and solving problems and which, last but not least, reject the social order in force. However, extremism can also be defined as a second-level approach to violent extremism and third-level terrorism, which has already been precisely defined. A further and final set of essential findings is an analysis of the current Slovenian legislation. On the basis of the material gathered and the testimony of the expert witnesses, we propose the following amendments to the law: Article 297 of the Penal Code, amendment to the law on the protection of public order and peace, amendment to the Law on the tasks and powers of the police, amendment to the law governing public order, or in the system law on offences, amendment to the law on public servants, amendment to the law on the financing of political parties and amendment to the Law on Media. In the interim report, the Commission of Inquiry has taken advantage of the business option and proposes to the National Assembly to adopt a decision to establish that both at European Union level and in individual Member States, with the Republic of Slovenia no exception, reinforce extremism. The national assembly shall propose, by a decision, to the competent authorities to prepare, in accordance with the above-mentioned proposals for amending legislation and the essential findings of the parliamentary inquiry, proposals for amendments or additions to legislation which mainly governs the field of criminal law, public order and peace, the police, breaches of public servants, political parties and the media. I propose that the National Assembly adopt the proposed decision. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2014-01-31-SDZ6-Redna-21.ana.u154	Colleagues and colleagues, the ones you've endured! The concept of extremism doesn't know our legal order. It is a social phenomenon that each country must take extremely seriously and thus approach it, especially in the light of acts that can result in violence. In modern history, extremism is primarily referred to as the ideology and actions that are directed against the rule of law and democracy and undermine constitutional and social order, as well as the emergence of nationalism and racism, anti-Semitism. It is undeniable that extremism is also present in the Republic of Slovenia. In this regard, I would like to draw attention to the need for each country and society to reach a consensus on which those extremist phenomena and groups should be monitored, limited and prevented. This group certainly includes terrorism as an extreme form of extremism, intolerance, xenophobia, racism and the emergence of hate speech, left, right and other extremism, and, of course, religious extremism. As you know, Positive Slovenia was at the time when it pointed out that the appearance and functioning of extremist groups in the Republic of Slovenia constituted a first-class political issue, rather lonely and had to resort to a so-called minority parliamentary investigation. The Law on Parliamentary Investigation allows a quarter of Members to submit a request on the basis of which a parliamentary inquiry is to be initiated without then voting on the National Assembly. This was also encouraged by the fact that the problems of extremism in the Republic of Slovenia have been avoided by the current authorities and ruling coalitions, whether left or right, for almost two decades, and thus left open the question of the existence of extreme groups in our country, allowing a wide range of their operations and inviolability. The purpose of the parliamentary investigation is not a political account either with the left or with right extremists, but a finding of the actual situation, as well as a search for an answer to the question whether an extremist group endangers state security and the social structure. Although the parliamentary commission of inquiry does not enjoy the reputation and trust of the public, this parliamentary inquiry has proved to be justified and necessary so far, in our opinion. In addition, unlike some of the investigative commissions so far, it is effective and focused on work rather than on political struggle. The latter, however, can be claimed only for those members of the commission who come from the ruling coalition. There was little talk about the commission’s work in public, even though some members of the commission made it difficult or tried to prevent the investigation from progressing. Members and presidents of the ruling coalition conducted the first two investigations without getting involved in senseless politicisation and even politics, which some members of the opposition commission in particular enjoyed. Thus far, the phenomenon and functioning of extremist groups has been tolerated and all of this on behalf of the right to unification and freedom of speech, and the greatest paradox in this regard is shown by the fact that freedom is expressed in accordance with the above-mentioned political tradition of liberalism, since the hate speech is "a category limited by the Constitution". In sanctioning an enemy speech, the State shall ensure public order and peace and the protection of vulnerable individuals or groups. However, it is also apparent that the provision of this parliamentary inquiry is satisfied only by the normative regulation of the part of the problem of extremism, which has proved to be flawed at the top of everything, that is not perceived or recognised by certain competent authorities implementing this legislation, such as the State Prosecutor's Office, the Ministry of Education and so on. This finding is crucial or essential for legislative changes, which means that the competent authorities, except intelligence and security authorities, such as the prosecutions, courts, approach separately, each in their own way to face this phenomenon and prosecute the crimes committed by individuals, members of extremist groups. Apart from terrorism, all crimes committed by extremist groups are not persecutable in the so-called association. Partial approaches without an appropriate single strategy and focus on prosecution alone have always proved unsuccessful. Based on the material collected so far and the testimony of expert witnesses, it is possible to establish that all national security authorities and expert witnesses determine the existence of extreme groups in Slovenia; both left and right, Islamic and national security threats. It is of concern, however, that some other competent state authorities or even competent government ministers have, in preparing the government report, assessed that Slovenia has legislation in the field of criminal law that reflects all modern and internationally established practices and standards, and the Minister refers only to the appropriateness of defining the most extreme form of extremism, namely terrorism. In this regard, it should be noted that Slovenia has faithfully transposed international commitments in this area only after the intervention of the international organisations to which it is a member. In addition, terrorism is only one possible strategy for the action of extremist groups, and it is not necessarily used by these groups at all. Therefore, a comprehensive regime, i.e. only one of the strategies of these groups, is insufficient in dealing with the phenomenon, because it does not cover the whole spectrum of the activities of these groups, and also in the event that these groups nevertheless decide to act terroristly, the police will probably not detect it in time because they do not have the authority to obtain and evaluate information on the operation of extremist groups. It would also be difficult, or at all, for the police to take preventive action in the case of the so-called Lone Actor, the lone wolf, or extreme right-wing man Anders Breivik in Norway, who killed 77 people in bombings and mass shootings. In Positive Slovenia, we point out that each and every individual case is too much and that it is a matter of endangering the safety of all of us, not only the affected individuals, because the goal of extremist groups is not individuals, but rather the social structure and state regulation as such. We are thus at risk for all members of society, and in general the purpose of these groups is to prevent or immediately sanction their operation. If there is an escalation or radicalisation of their operation, it will be too late or too much social damage. In Positive Slovenia, we hope that the change in the attitude of the competent state authorities and the competent minister towards the issue of extremism will be influenced, in addition to the findings of the commission of inquiry, by the calls of the European Commission, which called on members to take a firmer stand on the brink of violent extremism, as there is a risk that extremist and populist forces will be further strengthened by the European elections in 2014. Most of the EU's rules dealing with hostile, racist and xenophobic acts have not yet been properly transposed, not to mention the European Commission's report, which identifies 10 areas where Member States and the European Union should strengthen their measures to prevent all forms of extremism leading to violence, regardless of who supports it. In doing so, it is worth recalling that the conference and the meetings of the RAN are also attended by Slovenia or by the competent state authorities, but it appears so far that there are no more serious intentions. Finally, the problem of extremism in the Republic of Slovenia was also highlighted by public broadcasting, which in this week presented the operation and integration of extremist groups, including the names of some of their leaders. Citizens and citizens are entitled to know the facts and to know whether we can be concerned about this phenomenon. In view of the investigation carried out so far, it shows that the situation concerning instruments to combat this phenomenon and the functioning of national security authorities is much worse than has been made public so far. While the Slovenian legal order or legislation also contains provisions that provide even constitutional protection against discrimination, prejudice, violence and hatred, the majority of the professional public notes that the legislation in force does not allow for preventive control of action and effective action against the activities of extremist groups that promote the ideology of racism, xenophobia, anti-Semitism, homophobia and so on. Some countries are even prohibited from operating certain extremist groups. The question is how national security authorities should respond appropriately if they do not have the appropriate legal basis. However, before that, it is necessary to identify those groups, the movements whose activities constitute a threat to democracy, constitutional order and public order and peace. In the case of extremism, therefore, it is not the world view, the legitimate beliefs, but the facts, the actions against the rule of law and the democratic state that undermine its constitutional and social order. We cannot, of course, ignore the multi-disciplinary approach to dealing with extremism. We therefore agree in the parliamentary group with the key findings of the investigative commission and support the proposed legislative changes. In Positive Slovenia, we will also support the decision proposed by the Commission of Inquiry to find that both at European Union level and in individual Member States, the Republic of Slovenia is not an exception, strengthening extremism. It is important, however, that the competent authorities prepare, in accordance with those proposals, amendments to the legislation and the essential findings of the parliamentary inquiry, proposals for amendments or additions to the legislation already mentioned. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-SI_2014-01-31-SDZ6-Redna-21.ana.u158	Thank you for your word, Madam President. Nice hello to you all! There are fewer of us, and I hope that that's why those of us who have survived and remain are so much more interested in the subject that we are considering right now. The issue of extreme groups was addressed by the National Assembly in 2012, when it was first addressed by the Security and Intelligence Supervisory Commission, and in January 2013 a special investigative committee for this area was appointed. In determining its course of work, the Commission of Inquiry decided that the first part of its work should focus primarily on determining the extent of this phenomenon in Slovenia and how to regulate it legally and on this basis an interim report has also been drawn up. Social Democrats believe that the first phase of the Commission's action has shown a number of important features of the phenomenon of extremism in our country and the political will to deal with it so far. However, we believe that a final assessment will be possible after the second phase of the investigation, in which the answers to some of the questions and dilemmas we have detected in the first phase can be crystallized, but we have not yet found the appropriate answers to them. We also assess that the plenary debate in this report could show what the current political will is as regards the in-depth public debate on the problem of extreme groups, as well as whether we have enough political will to move forward with this dimension of security in Slovenia and Europe, or whether we will remain in the report and remain silent. We drew up the report on the basis of the findings from the two research tasks they had prepared in the National Electoral Research Service, then on the basis of the testimony of six expert witnesses, and on the basis of a discussion with the representatives of the security services in the Republic of Slovenia, on the basis of the findings of the Commission on Security and Intelligence Services, then on the basis of the documentation obtained by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia, then the reports of the Sova, OSCE, the Ombudsman and the Supreme Public Prosecutor's Office. We also used publicly available scientific and professional resources. We are aware that more resources and even more diopasone positions could be taken into account. After all, it is a subject that develops every day and we are again and again faced with the dilemma of whether radical political behaviour in the public is acceptable or not. Reports that we have had at our disposal often state that the government or its segments are subject to zero tolerance for extreme groups and their sympathizers, but whether it is a real practice, or whether it is a more comforting position for the public to ask what is happening in Slovenia, to use granite cubes against police and parliaments in front of Parliament, or to have an organized group of extremists with a Celtic cross on a banner, who are involved in provocations between demonstrators, or that there are also members of the Slovenian Armed Forces among detained bullies, or that the massacres following the monuments of Fascist violence in return are carried out by extremists from Slovenia. Social Democrats believe that, in our public and also in Parliament, there are too few in-depth and serious debates on the radicalisation of Slovenian society and that the emergence of extreme groups is being pushed into oblivion by both our consciousness and political measures. In assessing security, the State is encouraged by the findings of the intelligence and security agency that Slovenia’s level of risk with regard to extremist groups and trends is low. At the same time, in almost the same reports of the intelligence services and the police, we find that extremism is poorly defined in us, that we do not have the legal definition of extremism and that Article 297 of the Penal Code is used to sanction this phenomenon, which is so complicated that it is almost impossible to apply it in practice. In view of this situation, I argue that the security assessments of the relevant services may not reflect the overall security phenomenon of extreme groups and trends in Slovenia. I have no basis for doubting that the level of threat caused by extreme groups is low, but this assessment needs to be put in correlation with other, more primary forms of threat posed by modern society. All I'm saying is that the whole phenomenon of extremists in our security company may not have been detected. Why? First of all, because it is a question that only part of its appearance touches on the competence of security services. When it comes to violence, which is treated as a crime, or as an offence, or as an hate speech. Under our legislation, the security services do not have the authority to verify in advance the indications that a crime is being committed, but it is groups that are organised in the long term and are acting best in a society of universal ignorance and alienation. We are dealing with organisations that are very intelligently organised and carefully choose modes of action in which they do not come into contact with state institutions. For example, they are not organised as a society, they carry out their actions outside the reach of the public, i.e. at private parties and concerts, such as where invitations are personally and in the form of SMS. T-shirts and extreme symbols come from our schoolmates on unknown, generally friendly paths. Extreme groups also use methods that are characteristic of non-violence movements – such as: be and, sit and – members appear at certain public meetings and events where freedom of association is respected, they spend some time there, they alert themselves with their clothes, tattoos and inscriptions and disappear. In short, we will not find all these forms of action in police statistics on crimes and offences. It is therefore possible to question the assessment of the degree of presence of these trends in our society. This is also one of the open issues of this interim report, how much in fact there are already members of extreme groups in Slovenia, and of course not such ones with a membership card, but such, deeply indoctrinated, qualified and secularly convinced of their extreme ideologies and comrades in the group. How many of those young people in secondary and primary schools who may be in the recruitment phase for these movements right now? However, the reason for our youth to be radicalised is that we already have enough in the data on their social situation and social marginalisation as soon as they emerge from the compulsory educational process. One of the expert witnesses mentioned that there could be up to 150 people in our society who are ready to take any extreme action at any moment. That's supposed to be police data. And almost the same day, when the witness spoke about it, the police sent out a denial of his allegations to the public. You will find the controversy in the report in very detail. The question to me is why the police dementors. Is it because these prepared extremists are actually less or perhaps more substantial? However, in this same denial, the police are also hiding the answer to some other dilemmas. It tells us that we do not have a definition of extremism in Slovenia and Europol. So we're talking about a phenomenon that doesn't have a name. It is suppressed on the margins of our awareness, rather than on its emerging forms, we discuss and leave it in the hands of the security authorities, together with very impotent legislation in this area. We wrote down the definition of extremism in the report. But not a legal definition, but a generally understandable definition of extremism. We borrowed it from the scientific part of one of the Slovenian advocates, Dr. Andre Sotler, who wrote among the few of us about the problem of defining extremism. At the suggestion of the Committee member Dragutin Matei, we summarized the classification of the extreme groups through NATO and EU security studies. The Commission has also prepared a set of laws to be supplemented with concrete articles if we want to regulate the issue of extremism in a more appropriate manner. We believe that in addition to the legislative dimension of this problem, the educational or educational dimension is increasingly highlighted. The emerging forms and symbols of extremists must be taught by the whole society, but most of all the intelligence and security services, and we also assess the need to educate teachers in identifying extremists or in identifying recruitment for extremists in school premises. In schools there is still time and space for organised education on the problem of extremists. In this belief, we are also strengthened by the international debate on extremism. Done at Spiegel of 29. 1. 2014, this is two days ago, let us refer to the article on large-scale discussion in Germany about what to do with students who are members of neo-Nazi groups. Name them, embarrass them, ignore them, fire them from universities or prescribe them reeducational study programs? Practices are different. Also on the side of student organisations; sometimes they are neo-Nazi members of student leadership; elsewhere they publicly point to them in classrooms. They point out that they study the most often the law so that they can represent the rights of their comrades before the courts as their lawyers or often go to teaching professions so that they can pass on Nazi ideology to children. What do we do in Slovenia? We may also find answers to these questions at the second stage of the work of the commission of inquiry. In the Social Democrats Group, the conclusions of the report are fully supported and we hope that our National Assembly is not on the side of those who ignore the issue of extreme movements. However, we are aware that there are also discussions about these phenomenal swords. We may bring more fear to citizens who are already in enough trouble, or we may tell extremes that they are winning because we have noticed them and they are worth our attention. And to warn extremists is, they say, the best commercial for them. And that is also what Der Spiegel notes in this text. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2014-01-31-SDZ6-Redna-21.ana.u160	Thank you for your word, Mr. President. Good evening, everyone! I am sorry that I will have to read the position more quickly, for I do not have much time to announce. Your Honor! Although this is the position of the Group of Members, the opinion of the Group of Members is also reflected in my position as a member of the Commission of Inquiry for the determination and assessment of the activities of extremist groups. From the outset, I would like to announce that we will support the proposals for conclusions in the interim report in the Civil Gazette. Let me point out in the introduction that, despite some different views, this Commission will justify its existence, even if only one act of an individual or an extremist group is prevented, which could otherwise have occurred. And even though it would eventually prove that the Commission has achieved the most by launching a public debate on this, the objective will be positive, as its purpose will be reflected in an educational sense. That extremism and consequently extremist acts in Slovenia are not taboos, we are not only reminded of some of the events that took place as part of the legal protests that started in Maribor in 2012 and then spread to other Slovenian cities, but some of the acts that could be defined as extremist have occurred in our country before. The content work of the Parliamentary Commission is divided into two parts; the first part, which is also addressed in the interim report, refers to findings that could be the basis for possible legislative changes to prevent action and to control extremist groups. One of the priorities of the Commission was to make a proposal for the definition of extremism. The fact that this task is not simple speaks of the fact that only Slovakia is aware of the definition in the European Union, but we cannot go beyond the fact that, in fact, every single thing and every single act can be described in words. From this point of view, the arguments that they do not have definitions in other countries may also be irrelevant. The Commission has adopted today's proposal on the definition of extremism, which could also be the basis for addressing extremism from a legislative point of view. On the basis of all the data collected, we can say that Slovenia is not immune to certain types of extremism, as we have both left and right and religious movements with extremist tendencies. However, it should be stressed that the level of national security risk in all emerging forms is low, but we must not forget the particular type of danger posed by so-called lone wolves whose purposes are difficult to identify or control in advance. As a particular form of extremism, I would like to mention the hate speech, which is obviously the most common, but because of its intrusiveness and high freedom of expression, it is difficult to prove and persecutive, but nevertheless very dangerous. Online services, forums, social networks, online media are the generators of hate speech and control over them is very limited. In the interim report, I would like to draw particular attention to point 3.7, which discusses proposals for amending legislation and, under this point, to subparagraph 3.7.1, which refers to the Penal Code and its 297th Article, which defines the public promotion of hatred, violence or intolerance. The Commission proposes to supplement this Article, which has proved in practice to be difficult to disclose and consequently rarely to use. ParlaMint-SI_2014-01-31-SDZ6-Redna-21.ana.u164	Thank you for your word, Madam President. Dear colleagues and colleagues! At the beginning of the presentation of the DeSUS Group's position, let me briefly touch on the reasons for the participation of a member of our parliamentary group to participate in the Investigation Commission in identifying and assessing the activities of extremist groups in the Republic of Slovenia. Our primary purpose is to achieve, through the operation and conduct of investigations, the results of which will prove or disprove the facts, whether there are actually extremist groups in Slovenia, and if there are, what types of extremist groups exist, and not least how much real danger they pose to the political order of our country. The first part of the work of our commission of inquiry was also intended to obtain the most qualitative findings and assessments of the actual situation in the country, which could be the basis for amending legislation to prevent the operation and control of extremist groups. With the current ten-month active work of the commission of inquiry, we have reached half of our work at the seven sessions so far, this is the final drafting of the draft interim report. In this part of its operation, the Commission of Inquiry received material from nine bodies summarizing the state of extremism in Slovenia. We also took note of the research task of extremist organisations and their functioning, as well as of regulatory arrangements in 15 other countries. In addition, the Commission obtained material from the following national authorities: Slovenian Intelligence and Security Services, Ministry of Defence Intelligence and Security Services, the Ombudsman, the State Attorney's Office, the police, the Ministry of Justice, the Ministry of Education, Science and Sport and the Commission of the National Intelligence and Security Services. Within the evidence procedure, the Commission of Inquiry also listened to statements by six expert witnesses interviewed by recognised experts, including prominent members of Slovenian universities. In preparing the draft interim report, however, I must certainly not miss the important professional assistance of our external expert investigative committee Dr. Peter Umk, who is certainly known in public as an exceptional specialist in criminal psychology. Its expertise in the prepared interim report is large, in particular its essential findings. The main part of the interim report contains the essential findings of the Commission and I should say that most of the members of the investigative commission support the interim report and that a separate opinion has been tabled by three members, so I expect the report to be adopted by the National Assembly. At the very beginning of the report, the Commission is of the opinion that the comprehensive problems of extremism in the Republic of Slovenia have been avoided by all the authorities since independence, leaving an open question about the existence of extreme groups in our country, thereby allowing for a wide range of action and inviolability of these groups, all on behalf of the right to unify and freedom of speech. Based on the material collected so far and the testimony of expert witnesses, it is possible to conclude that all national security authorities, expert witnesses, determine the existence of extreme groups in Slovenia both left and right and religious. In addition, I must point out that the current social and economic situation of water per mill is for the operation and activities of extremist groups both in our country and throughout the European Union, where their operation is increasing. But the undeniable fact is that extremist groups also exist in our country. These are organised and actually pose a risk, but for the time being, according to the assessment of our competent national authorities, pose a lower level of risk to national security. While Slovenian legislation also contains provisions that provide comprehensive constitutional protection against discrimination, prejudice, violence and hatred, the majority of the professional public notes that the legislation in force does not allow for preventive control of action and effective action against the actions of extremist groups that promote the ideology of racism, xenophobia, anti-Semitism and homophobia. In view of this fact, given the difficulties identified in dealing with the phenomenon of extremism in the Republic of Slovenia, certain interventions into existing legislation should also be considered. Above all, the practice of detection, prevention and effective prosecution or punishment must be implemented in this area. Of course, before that, what are those groups and movements whose operation poses a threat to democracy, constitutional order and public order and peace? Therefore, this moment raises the question of how state and security authorities should respond appropriately if they do not have the appropriate legal basis. When searching for the definition of extremism, the commission of inquiry tried first to present extremism and extremist movements. In doing so, she found that we do not have a generally established and recognized definition of extremism in the Republic of Slovenia. That conclusion, however, is crucial to the need to amend existing legislation. This means that the competent national authorities are now taking a separate approach, each in their own way, to dealing with this phenomenon and the prosecution of crimes caused by extremist groups or individuals by members of extremist groups. In doing so, it should be noted that, apart from terrorism, the remaining crimes of extremist groups are not at all persecutable in so-called associations. Otherwise, the activities of these groups should be prevented or immediately sanctioned. So it's a prevention, and it's a curate. In the Republic of Slovenia today there are so many left-wing, right-wing and religious extremists. Left-wing anarchists in our country do not pose a threat to constitutional regulation. Right-wing extremism is not, in principle, a threat to national security, but it can change if it receives more active support from political actors. Islamic extremism in our country, according to the assessment of the security authorities, is not so explicit as it is not detected by organisational groups, but there are militant individuals who could pose a security risk. In her work, the Commission of Inquiry also gave special emphasis to the so-called hate speech. The hate speech is an expression of opinions and ideas which, by their nature, are discriminatory, xenophobic, racist, homophobic and similar and are directed at different minorities: ethnic, national, religious, cultural, sexual and other. In doing so, the Commission notes that a democratic and tolerant society must allow its members to express and defend a wide range of views and ideas, both publicly and privately, even though they may seem provocative, stupid or even sometimes offensive. However, in cases where views and ideas affect the rights of individuals, society must restrict and protect the rights of the individual. The Commission of Inquiry thus offers a broader definition of extremism, which reads as follows: "Extremism is an expression that denotes activities that are morally, ideologically or politically contrary to written laws, both legal and constitutional, and unwritten norms of the country that are extremely impatient with others, reject democracy as a means of governance and a way to solve problems and which, finally, reject the current social order. However, extremism can also be defined as a second-level approach to violent extremism and third-level terrorism, which is already precisely defined." I am of the opinion that the Commission of Inquiry has done its job well so far and that the debate of the National Assembly, in addition to, of course, the content of this interim report itself, will be more than a good basis for the drafting of the necessary new laws, which should be amended in the opinion of the Commission of Inquiry. This interim report is also an excellent basis for the further successful work of our Commission. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2014-01-31-SDZ6-Redna-21.ana.u189	Thank you very much. Maja Dimitrovsky, you have the floor.
ParlaMint-SI_2014-01-31-SDZ6-Redna-21.ana.u190	Thank you so much for saying that. First, Mr Pukšič, as well as you, Mr Jerovšek, would be asked to write down my last name, because my last name is Dimitrovski and not Dimitrovska. You've called me twice as Dimitrovska in your debate. I would also like to ask Mrs Potrato, who is with us, to perhaps teach you how my last name is removed. So much for a start. In my debate, I will focus mainly on the essential findings, but of course I will not be able to pass the debate on a separate opinion. On the Commission we proposed the following amendments to the legislation: Article 297 of the Penal Code to be supplemented by incriminating acts committed with discriminatory motives. The Penal Code should also be supplemented by criminal offences where the victim of integrity is injured because of national, religious, racial or other hatred. The law on the protection of public order and peace should also regulate and sanction the international phenomenon of private gatherings or meetings of extremist groups where participants in concerts, symbols, conduct express and disseminate their discriminatory and impatient ideology. In the Law on Police Tasks and Powers, it would indicate, having regard to the Constitutional Court's decision, that additional powers would allow the police to gather information at the so-called early stage – to gather evidence to confirm that extremist groups are preparing possible crimes, including those referred to in Article 297 of the Penal Code. In a law governing the area of public order or in a system-wide law on offences, it should be possible to add the possibility that extremists who have committed multiple offences may immediately pass a fine. The police should keep a special record of this. In the field of public servants legislation, public servants should be prohibited from cooperating with extremist groups, which should be a condition for the conclusion of employment, as well as for the extraordinary cancellation. In the Law on the Financing of Political Parties, the parties should assume financial responsibility for the conduct of their officials and elected members in representative functions. In particular, attention would be paid to preventing the promotion of hatred, violence and intolerance, taking the financial penalty away from the budgetary financing of political parties and depriving the resources of preventing hatred, violence and intolerance. In the Media Act, the responsibility of the media or responsible editors for comments on articles or contributions that promote or promote hatred, violence and intolerance should be extended. At the same time, the competent inspection services should be empowered to take appropriate action or to impose penalties or fines. We have given special attention to the so-called hate speech in the Commission, because hate speech is one of the most powerful tools of discrimination, and it is based on the belief that some people are less valuable because they belong to a particular group because of individual circumstances. The main objective of the hate speech is to humanize those against whom it is directed, and their aim is to humiliate, intimidate or incite violence or to engage in illegal acts against a particular person or group. In Slovenia, the problem of hate speech is also related to the rhetoric of some political officials and we are all witnesses to it. One more word about right-wing extremism. As stated in the report, right-wing extremism can most generally be described as the denial of the right to alternative ideas, while right-wing extremists stress that their ideas are only correct. Social values understand through the prism we-they. The fundamental aspects of right extremism are populism and excessive devotion, loyalty to one group, conspiracy theory or secret plans of the so-called enemy of society. The rhetoric of right-wing extremists is generally directed at a selected group of people who become guilty of all irregularities and injustices in society. In the past, they performed with various emblems — swastika, shaved heads, and black garments. Now their presentation and communication are much more sophisticated. They gather in certain places, organize concerts, which are not public, of course, and have contact with related organisations abroad. The principles of right-wing extremism do not present a threat to national security at this time, but it can change if it receives more active support from political actors. On the left, extremist researchers say that extreme left extremism advocates the theory of violence, fear, assassinations, kidnapping, violent demonstrations. Left extremists are convinced that reform of the system or revolution is necessary to create a just society. There are extremist leftist groups that have radical political positions, but they do not usually choose violence or call for violence for their political strategy. In the vast majority of cases, they act nonviolently, for example, against the system by retreating into a ghetto where they try to practice ideal social relations. With us left-wing anarchists do not pose any threat to constitutional regulation. We can say about religious Islamic extremism that its violence and the call to violence are equivalent to any other extremism. Religious extremism in some way means politicising faith to achieve a collective goal, subjugating the entire social order to religious values. The latter leads to a constant religious struggle to enforce religious commands. In this respect, Islamic fundamentalism, which poses a growing threat, is particularly problematic with the European Union. In Slovenia, based on the assessment of the security authorities, Islamic extremism is not so explicit as it is not detected by organised groups, but there are, of course, individuals who may pose a security risk. The most difficult task of this investigative commission was the definition of extremism and is an important part of the essential findings, as our legal order is unknown. Finally, let me touch on a separate opinion, as I mentioned in the introduction. First, I can say that the report was adopted after several days of lengthy and very thorough debate. We have completed the draft report extensively, as four pages of the minutes of this meeting also bear witness, yet today we are listening to how the opinion of the opposition has not been taken into account. I don't think that's true. Despite the fact that the report was not unanimously adopted, it should be pointed out that the essential findings are based on the beliefs of experts, on established foreign practices and on the proposals of the Security Intelligence Authorities. A key part of the report was contributed by Prof. Peter Umek, a recognised specialist in criminal psychology and a forensic expert. In view of the political sensitivity of the subject matter, the Commission has limited itself to the findings and proposals. We have avoided content that could be attributed to political motivation, but today we are receiving unjustifiable evidence of political bias. On the basis of the above facts, it is therefore possible to express both surprise and regret that the members of the opposition commission decided to oppose and draw up a separate opinion on the interim report, thus rejecting all the collected facts which could not be attributed to political interpretations. If I simplify, they have discredited the state authorities, the government and even the experts who have been dealing with this issue and have been monitoring it for a long time.
